## Document 005: 115年8月海關進出口貿易初步統計 (2026-09-09)

Report source: MOF
Date: 2026-09-09
Pages: 8
Extraction status: ok

### Page 1

115 年 8 月海關進出口貿易初步統計 
一、進出口貿易變動 
（一）出口 
8 月出口 824.0 億美元，創歷年單月新高，較上月增 9.4%，較上年
同月增 41.0%(按新臺幣計算增 52.6%)；累計 1 至 8 月出口 5,743.4
億美元，較上年同期增 44.2%(按新臺幣計算增 46.5%)。 
（二）進口 
8 月進口 601.0 億美元，為歷年單月第 3 高，較上月增 3.4%，較上
年同月增 44.3%(按新臺幣計算增 56.2%)；累 計1 至 8 月進口 4,374.3
億美元，較上年同期增 40.4%(按新臺幣計算增 42.4%)。 
（三）出入超 
8 月出超 223.0 億美元，為單月新高，較上年同月增 55.2 億美元；
累計 1 至 8 月出超 1,369.1 億美元，較上年同期增 500.5 億美元。

### Page 2

2 
二、出口主要貨品 
（一）與上年同月比較 
受惠於人工智慧(AI)、 高效能運算及雲端服務等應用持續擴增，相關
產品出貨維持強勁， 加上價格攀升效 應，8月出口較上年同月增 239.7
億美元(+41.0%)，主要變動如下： 
1. 電子零組件出口 322.2 億美元(歷年單月新高)， 較上年同月增118.3
億美元(+58.0%)，其中積體電路增 115.5 億美元(+60.0%)、印刷電
路增 1.4 億美元(+29.9%)、 電容器及電阻器增0.7 億美元(+24.9%)。 
2. 資通與視聽產品出口 331.0 億美元，較上年同月增 97.6 億美元
(+41.8%)，其中電腦及其附屬單元增68.7 億美元(+40.4%)、儲存媒
體增 19.1 億美元(+2.4 倍)、 交換器及路由器增9.7 億美元(+1.1 倍)。 
3. 礦產品出口 20.0 億美元，較上年同月增 8.3 億美元(+70.2%)，其
中石油煉製品增 7.8 億美元(+74.3%)。 
4. 基本金屬及其製品出口 29.6 億美元，較上年同月增 5.4 億美元
(+22.4%)，其中銅及其製品增 6.1 億美元(+1.5 倍)，鋼鐵及其製品
減 1.1 億美元(-8.7%)。 
5. 機械出口 26.8 億美元，較上年同月增 3.6 億美元(+15.2%)，其中
生產半導體等機械增 1.9 億美元(+42.1%)、未列名具特殊功能機械
增 0.4 億美元(+39.0%)。

累計 1 至 8 月出口較上年同期增 1,759.8 億美元(+44.2%)，以資通與
視聽產品增 1,023.7 億美元(+68.5%)、電子零組件增 629.5 億美元
(+45.8%)增加較多。

生產等出現結構性轉變，爰另予併計。

三、進口主要貨品 
（一）與上年同月比較 
由於 AI 產業鏈國際分工與出口衍生需求之帶動，積體電路及資通產
品進口維持強勁，加上輸入物價明顯上漲，8 月總進口較上年同月增
184.5 億美元(+44.3%)，主要變動如下： 
1. 農工原料進口408.4 億美元， 較上年同月增129.4億美元(+46.4%)，
其中電子零組件增 82.5 億美元(+62.6%)、礦產品增 16.9 億美元
(+38.4%)、電腦之零附件增 14.6 億美元(+84.4%)、基本金屬增 4.5
億美元(+21.8%)、塑化原料增 4.0 億美元(+16.2%)。 
2. 資本設備進口 130.6 億美元， 較上年同月增 38.4 億美元(+41.6%)，
其中資通設備增 27.2 億美元(+1.2 倍)、半導體設備增 2.8 億美元
(+7.3%)、交通運輸設備增 2.8 億美元(+1.1 倍)。 
3. 消費品進口 55.7 億美元，較上年同月增 16.0 億美元(+40.5%)，其
中固態非揮發性儲存裝置增12.1 億美元(+3.7 倍)、藥粧用品增 1.2
億美元(+17.4%)、手機增 1.1 億美元(+78.1%)。

四、主要出口國家/地區 
（一）與上年同月比較 
8 月對陸港、日本出口創新高，對美國、東協為單月第 3 高 
1. 對美國出口 236.7 億美元，較上年同月增 40.6 億美元(+20.7%)，
其中資通與視聽產品增 23.0 億美元(+14.6%)、電子零組件增 11.6
億美元(+1.5 倍)、電機產品增 1.7 億美元(+30.1%)。 
2. 對中國大陸與香港出口 211.5 億美元，較上年同月增 59.7 億美元
(+39.3%)，其中電子零組件增 47.4 億美元(+45.6%)、資通與視聽
產品增 6.7 億美元(+42.9%)、 基本金屬及其製品增4.6 億美元(+1.2
倍)。 
3. 對東協出口 151.3 億美元，較上年同月增 44.9 億美元(+42.2%)，
其中電子零組件增 25.3 億美元(+50.3%)、資通與視聽產品增 12.6
億美元(+41.1%)、礦產品增 4.6 億美元(+1.5 倍)。 
4. 對歐洲出口 44.5 億美元，較上年同月增 11.5 億美元(+34.8%)，其
中資通與視聽產品增 6.6 億美元(+57.0%)、電子零組件增 4.0 億美
元(+1.1 倍)，基本金屬及其製品減 1.4 億美元(-31.0%)。 
5. 對日本出口 36.6 億美元，較上年同月增8.9 億美元(+32.0%)，其中
電子零組件增 8.9 億美元(+76.7%)、 化學品增0.5 億美元(+32.3%)，
資通與視聽產品減 0.6 億美元(-13.9%)。 
（二）與上年同期比較 
累計 1 至 8 月對美國出口 1,803.2 億美元，較上年同期增 631.6 億美
元(+53.9%)，對中國大陸與香港出口 1,394.7 億美元，增 307.5 億美
元(+28.3%)， 對東協出口1,150.7 億美元 ， 增372.3 億美元(+47.8%)，
對歐洲出口 376.6 億美元 ， 增130.7 億美元(+53.1%)， 對日本出口247.0
億美元，增 54.7 億美元(+28.4%)。

五、主要進口國家/地區 
（一）與上年同月比較 
8 月自中國大陸與香港進口 126.8 億美元 ， 較上年同月增47.6 億美元
(+60.2%)，自東協進口 106.9 億美元，增46.7 億美元(+77.6%)，自南
韓進口 91.7 億美元，增29.6 億美元(+47.7%)，自日本進口 57.0 億美
元，增 11.2 億美元(+24.4%)，自美國進口 53.9 億美元，增 14.3 億美
元(+35.9%)，自歐洲進口 49.5 億美元，增 6.2 億美元(+14.3%)。 
（二）與上年同期比較 
累計 1 至 8 月自主要國家/地區進口與上年同期比較 ， 自東協增75.4%、
自南韓增 63.2%、自中國大陸與香港增 40.5%、自美國增 24.4%、自
日本增 24.1%、自歐洲增 12.8%。

六、主要國家/地區貿易概況 
（一）今年 1-7 月主要國家/地區外貿景氣普遍上升，南韓(1-8 月)、香港、
新加坡出口動能尤為強勁，分別年增 52.8%、46.6%、38.3%；中國
大陸(1-8 月)、美國出口分別年增 19.3%及 14.6%，德國(1-6 月)、日
本各年增 10.9%、8.1%。 
（二）主要國家/地區進口同步上揚，累計 1-7 月以香港進口年增 48.1%最
為顯著， 新加坡、 中國大陸(1-8 月)分別年增 37.0%、27.0%，南韓(1-8
月)、德國(1-6 月)各增 18.7%、11.7%，日本、美國各增 6.5%、1.2%。

圖 5 2026 年主要國家/地區出進口年增率
七、綜合分析
（一）得益於人工智慧(AI)、高效能運算及 雲端服務等應用 需求強勁， 加
以外銷產品價格攀升，8 月出口 824.0 億美元，創歷年單月新高，年
增 41.0%，連續 34 個月正成長；進口 601.0 億美元，年增 44.3%，
主因 AI 產業鏈國際分工與出口衍生需求擴張 ，及農工原料行情上
漲；出、進口互抵後，出超 223.0 億美元，亦創單月新高 。累計 1
至 8 月出口年增 44.2%、進口增 40.4%。
（二）8 月出口主要貨品以電子零組件、資通與視聽產品貢獻最多，年增率
分別為 58.0%及 41.8%，前者受惠 AI 商機及記憶體外銷價量齊揚，規
模值創單月新高，兩類併計增 49.3%，其餘貨類平均增 16.3%，以礦
產品隨油品售價上漲，年增 70.2%較為突出，基本金屬及其製品、電
機產品各增 22.4%、22.3%。累計 1 至 8 月資通與視聽產品出口值已
超越歷年水準，與電子零組件合計占總出口比重達 78.7%。

（三）8 月對主要市場出口同呈雙位數成長，其中對中國大陸與香港、日本
受電子零組件外銷強勁推升，出口值同創單月新高，各年增 39.3%、
32.0%，對東協、歐洲、美國分別增 42.2%、34.8%、20.7%；五大市
場之外，對墨西哥及澳大利亞出口皆增逾 2 倍。累計 1 至 8 月對美
國、歐洲出口各年增 53.9%、53.1%，對東協年增 47.8%，對日本、
陸港各增 28.4%、28.3%，對五大市場出口規模均為歷年同期最佳。
（四）展望未來，地緣政治衝突 及美國關稅政策變化等不確定 因素，持續
干擾全球經濟成長力道，惟隨人工智慧應用 不斷深化，算力需求攀
升，國際雲端服務業者加速建置 AI 基礎設施，加以國內半導體及伺
服器等供應鏈具競爭優勢，均有助支撐我國外銷動能，下半年出口
可望維持成長態勢。

投資評等 目標價 
買進 $800 
前次投資建議：2026.07.13： 
「買進」 ，目標價: $710

報告日期：2026.10.13 研 究 員：王穎

摘 要 及 調 整 原 因 
➢ 看好記憶體需求緊俏有利報價走升，維持「買進」 投資評等，目標價 800 元(6 x 27(F)PER)，
潛在上漲空間 51.8%。

基本資料 結論與建議 
2026/10/12 收盤價： 527.00 ➢ 26Q3 營收 1336.5 億元新台幣， QoQ+61.9%，YoY+ 
611.7%，毛利率 85.7% ，營業費用 75.88 億元，營業利
益為 1068.9億元 ， 業外收入24.38億 ， 稅後淨利為 876.3
億元﹐EPS 為 25.4 元，每股淨值為 118.99元。營收成
長及毛利雙雙成長受惠 平均銷售單價季增超過 六十位數
百分比，而季減中個位數百分比，匯率有利低個位數百分
比。 
➢ 展望 26Q4，受惠 AI 需求續強預期 DRAM 將持續缺貨，
且南亞科 AI相關產品已佔營收逾 20%，雖受限產能滿載
出貨量將無法再提升，然而預期合約價將持續於 26Q4上
漲 15~20%，營收及毛利率將持續創高。因此兆豐預估營
收 1556.04 億元，QoQ+16.4%，YoY+417.1%，毛利率 
88.1%，EPS為 30.35 元。 
➢ 展望 2026~2027 年，受惠 AI 需求持續強勁三大原廠於
DRAM產能持續滿載，預期 DDR5、DDR4、DDR3等產
品將持續供不應求。1C 產品預期於 27Q1 開始驗證，有
利打入 AI伺服器供應鏈，預期 AI 占比將逐季提高。因此
兆豐預估營收 4208.9/6993.1 億元，YoY+532.1/66.2%，
毛利率 83.3/86.3%，EPS 為 77.85/133.2 元。南亞科目
前位於(3.9 X 27(F) PER)，看好記憶體需求緊俏報價持續
走升， 維持「買進」投資評等，目標價  800 元(6 x 
27(F)PER)，潛在上漲空間 51.8%。

營 運 分 析

南亞科技為全球前四大 DRAM廠商 
南亞科技主要致力 DRAM (動態隨機存取記憶體)的研發、設計、製造與銷售，
開發自主技術，發展智慧財產權，培育關鍵技術研發團隊，落實綠色科技及
智慧製造全面提升製程效能與產能，是全球第4大的 DRAM公司。公司已成
功開發 10 奈米級 DRAM 製程技術，將可微縮至少 3 個世代，並用以發展
DDR5及 LPDDR5 等下世代產品，強化公司自主技術開發與製程轉換佈局。
第二世代 10 奈米級製程技術(1B)產品 16Gb DDR5 已完成設計與試產，於
2024 年量產。南亞科技具備完整 DRAM 產品線，可因應智慧世代的發展，
滿足多元化的產品需求。預期 1C產品於 27Q1 開始驗證。

個股報告 
26Q3 ASP成長帶動營收毛利雙雙上升 
26Q3 營收  1336.5 億元新台幣， QoQ+61.9%，YoY+ 611.7%，毛利率
85.7% ，營業費用 75.88 億元，營業利益為 1068.9 億元，業外收入 24.38
億，稅後淨利為 876.3 億元﹐EPS 為 25.4 元，每股淨值為 118.99元。營
收成長及毛利雙雙成長受惠平均銷售單價季增超過六十位數百分比，而季減
中個位數百分比，匯率有利低個位數百分比。

26Q4合約價持續上漲  
展望 26Q4，受惠 AI 需求續強預期 DRAM 將持續缺貨，且南亞科 AI 相關產
品已佔營收逾 20%，雖受限產能滿載出貨量將無法再提升，然而預期合約價
將持續於 26Q4 上漲 15~20%，營收及毛利率將持續創高。因此兆豐預估營
收 1556.04 億元，QoQ+16.4%，YoY+417.1%，毛利率 88.1%，EPS 為 30.35
元。

圖三、DRAM合約價報價走勢圖

資料來源：兆豐投顧整理

南亞科完成 787.2 億元私募案，攜手四大客戶搶佔 AI 供應鏈先機 
南亞科日前完成總額新台幣  787.2 億元的私募認購，吸引四大核心客戶參
與。增資後，相關投資方持股比例達 10.19%。雙方同時簽署供應協議，確
保未來能穩定供應多元 DRAM 產品組合。主要目的在於強化南亞科與客戶
的戰略合作，透過 e-SSD 與網通產品等領域，建立 AI 及雲端服務供應商
（CSP）供應鏈中的雙贏局面。私募資金將注資新廠以提升產能，進一步確
立南亞科在 AI 價值鏈中的市場地位。本次私募後，南亞科總發行股數將達 
34.5 億股。

記憶體供不應求，產能持續擴充 
目前產能為 6.5 萬片/月，產能利用率維持滿載，2026Q1~Q3 資本支出 276
億，2026 以不超過 697 億元為上限，設備佔比五成．5A新廠第一階段 35,900
片/月 於 2026~2029 資本支出約 3,466 億．新廠預計於 2027 年初開始裝機，
主要生產 1B/1C/1D製程，預期 27年底開出 20k/月，2028 年達每月投片量
達 30k/月，主要因應網通、eSSD等產品之強勁需求。

AI 帶動記憶體強勁需求，有利報價持續走升 
展望 2026~2027 年，受惠 AI 需求持續強勁三大原廠於 DRAM 產能持續滿
載，預期DDR5、DDR4、DDR3等產品將持續供不應求。1C產品預期於 27Q1
開始驗證，有利打入 AI 伺服器供應鏈，預期 AI 占比將逐季提高。因此兆豐
預估營收 4208.9/6993.1 億元，YoY+532.1/66.2%，毛利率83.3/86.3%，EPS
為 77.85/133.2 元。

記憶體持續供不應
求，報價持續走升。 
2027 新產能主要 用
於生產 1B/1C/1D 製
程。 
大型私募案確保未來
穩定供應多元 DRAM
產品組合。

### Page 1

ab 15 October 2026
Global Research
First Read
Genius Electronic Optical
Private placement with 3M expands its optical
connectivity opportunity
Genius approved a private placement with 3M
On 15th Oct, Genius's board approved a private placement to 3M UK Limited for
5.54mn shares at NT$869.6 per share, an 18.3% discount to the 14th Oct closing price
NT$1,065, representing NT$4.8bn investment and 4.68% stake in Genius. Proceeds
from the transaction will fund technology development, working capital, and bank loan

repayment. The two companies also announced a strategic partnership to develop
optical communication for AI data center and HPC. We believe this announcement is a
positive validation for Genius working with a tier one supplier in optical connectivity to
provide its precision components for advanced optics.
3M EBO technology could open up a US$100mn TAM for Genius
3M has been developing its EBO (expanded beam optical) technology since 2019 with
commercialization in late 2024 and deployment for Microsoft Azure data centers in July

2026. EBO is an upgrade to MPO connectors at the ends of fiber optics cables.  The
EBO is a lensed, non-contact ferrule that uses a prism lens to expand and collimate a
light beam to send across fiber before refocusing into a receiving fiber, improving dust
tolerance to cut cleaning steps and cable inspection times 85%. The technology was
supported by the MSA launched in May 2026, co-chaired by Oracle, with Meta, Senko
and Molex among the members for scale-out cabling. We saw opportunity opened for

Genius on the EBO ferrule, a small molded plastic part with a row of micro-lenses to
widen the light beam plus an anti reflection coating. This could open up a US$100mn
market TAM for Genius based on 4-12 ferrules per GPU (2-3 connections x 2-4 plugs per
connection) x 20mn accelerators x US$1 for the lens assembly.
Validation for its optical solution push with potential hyperscaler exposure
We believe the scale of placement at NT$4.8bn suggests scope to expand into CPO

where it could improve the MT ferrule used as the detachable plug end of the FAU to
hold the fiber in position when it is connected to a switch adapter or removable cable.
Genius  collaboration with 3M can bring the company closer to the end demand with its
direct partnership with Microsoft and bring revenue contribution forward to 2027,
ahead of Genius CPO ramp in 2028E. For CPO, we still see potential to reach 10% of the
TAM supplying MT ferrule, prism and V-groove.

Reiterate Buy with PT at NT$1,250
Our recent update on Genius had raised the PT from NT$700 to NT$1,250 reflecting the
company's potential CPO market with its progress sampling three precision components
inside the fiber array unit (FAU) targeting FAU integrators such as Coherent and FOCI.
The collaboration strengthens our confidence on Genius capability within the optical
connectivity industry, and reiterate our Buy on the compony.

Company Description
Founded in 1989, Genius Electronic Optical (GSEO) is a leading global manufacturer of
camera lenses, providing full service optical solutions, including mobile lenses, automotive
lenses and AR/VR optics. Its production sites are in Taichung (one plant) and Xiamen (two

plants). The Taichung plant mainly manufactures VR lenses, while the Xiamen plants produce
compact lenses for consumer electronics. Optical lens products accounted for 73% of 2023
total revenue, and others include TOF lenses, flash lenses, VR lenses and LED lighting
products. Its major client is Apple and it supplies lenses for iPhones and iPads.
UBS EPS and Consensus EPS
The UBS EPS is an adjusted diluted EPS metric. It is calculated using the UBS analyst's

interpretation of earnings suitable for valuation purposes divided by the diluted number of
shares. This may differ to the way the consensus EPS metric has been calculated.
Valuation Method and Risk Statement
Our price target is based on PE-based sum-of-the-parts model.
Genius' growth is highly leveraged to the overall handset industry and could be affected by
volatility in handset demand, as well as whether handset OEMs will continue upgrading their

camera design. Genius' margins could be affected by increasing competition and the rise of
new technologies, ASP pressure from customers and fluctuating material and labour costs.
Downside risks include competitors catching up in technology, yield rate issues for new
products and uncertain iPhone demand.

KEY DEFINITIONS:Forecast Stock Return (FSR) is defined as expected percentage price appreciation plus gross dividend yield over
the next 12 months. In some cases, this yield may be based on accrued dividends. Market Return Assumption (MRA) is defined as
the one-year local market interest rate plus 5% (a proxy for, and not a forecast of, the equity risk premium). Under Review (UR)
Stocks may be flagged as UR by the analyst, indicating that the stock's price target and/or rating are subject to possible change in the

near term, usually in response to an event that may affect the investment case or valuation. Equity Price Targets have an investment
horizon of 12 months.

Investing in structured investments involves significant risks. For a detailed discussion of the risks involved in investing in any particular structured investment, you must read

the relevant offering materials for that investment. Structured investments are unsecured obligations of a particular issuer with returns linked to the performance of an
underlying asset. Depending on the terms of the investment, investors could lose all or a substantial portion of their investment based on the performance of the underlying
asset. Investors could also lose their entire investment if the issuer becomes insolvent. UBS does not guarantee in any way the obligations or the financial condition of any

issuer or the accuracy of any financial information provided by any issuer. Structured investments are not traditional investments and investing in a structured investment is
not equivalent to investing directly in the underlying asset. Structured investments may have limited or no liquidity, and investors should be prepared to hold their investment
to maturity. The return of structured investments may be limited by a maximum gain, participation rate or other feature. Structured investments may include call features and,

if a structured investment is called early, investors would not earn any further return and may not be able to reinvest in similar investments with similar terms. Structured
investments include costs and fees which are generally embedded in the price of the investment. The tax treatment of a structured investment may be complex and may differ
from a direct investment in the underlying asset. UBS and its employees do not provide tax advice. Investors should consult their own tax advisor about their own tax situation

before investing in any securities.

時碩 (4566  TT)  Trading Buy

2H26 營運明顯增長 ，投資建議 Trading Buy 。  
投 資 建 議 Trading Buy ： 雖 目 前 時碩 (4566 TT) 處於 CB 緘 默 期 ，
未 提 供 明 確 展 望 ， 但 受 惠 於 航 太 、 散 熱 產 品 出 貨 增 長 ，3Q26 營
運 已 明 顯 攀 升 。 考 量 2026 、 2027 年 營 運 動 能 向 上 ， 投 資 建 議
Trading Buy ， 目 標 價 92 元 (以 202 7 年 EPS 及 PE  17 倍 評 價 )。  
時 碩 積 極 布 局 高 成 長 潛 力 的 利 基 型 市 場 ， 包 含 液 冷 散 熱 系 統 、
人 形 機 器 人 零 組 件 ： 液冷散熱 方面， 時 碩 致 力 於 提 供 一 站 式 的
全 方 位 熱 管 理 解 決 方 案 ， 其 產 品 線 涵 蓋 快 接 頭 、 分 歧 管 、 冷 卻
液 分 配 單 元 、 微 通 道 板 及 技 術 門 檻 較 高 的 水 冷 板 ， 並 能 提 供 不
鏽 鋼 與 銅 等 多 樣 材 質 。公司 已 成 功 獲 得 美 、 日 系 液 冷 系 統 大 廠
訂單，且於 2H26 放量 出貨 ， 預估 散 熱 產 品 佔 202 6 年 營 收 比 重
有望 接近 10% 。 人 形 機 器 人方 面 ， 時 碩 積 極 拓 展 此 領 域 的 關 鍵
零 組 件 業 務 ， 產 品 涵 蓋 諧 波 減 速 機 關 鍵 零 組 件 、 機 器 手 臂 關 節
精 密 機 械 加 工 零 件 。 時 碩 目 前 已 打 入 美 國 與 中 國 廠 商 的 供 應 鏈
體 系 ； 中 國 無 錫 廠 藉 由 地 緣 優 勢 積 極 切 入 關 節 零 件 製 造 。 泰 國
春 武 里 新 廠也 規 劃 將 產 線 逐 步 延 伸 至 機 器 人 相 關 應 用 領 域 。 
3Q26 營 收 明 顯 躍 升，預估 2026 、2027 年 營 運 動 能 向 上：雖 1H26
獲 利 表 現 平 平 ， 但 受 惠 於 航 太 、 散 熱 應 用 出 貨 明 顯 增 長 帶 動 ，
3Q26 營收 18.18 億元， QoQ+23.8 6%、 YoY+43.23% ； 且 出 貨 動
能 有 望 延 伸 至 4Q26 。2026 年 方 面 ，汽 車 、 航 太 、 工 業 三 大 領 域
皆有望較 2025 年 增 長。汽 車 應 用 方 面， 雖 中 國 市 場 競 爭 仍 然 激
烈，但 因 切 入 新 能 源 車 領 域 且 客 戶 需 求 帶 動 下，營 收穩 定 增 長。
航太領域 方面， 天 陽 航 太 已 通 過 多 項 航 太 認 證 ， 成 功 打 入 全 球
前 兩 大 飛 機 製 造 商 及 Tier 1 供 應 商 ， 預 估 營 收 將維 持 高 速 成 長
態勢 。 工 業 應 用 方 面 ， 既 有 領 域 有 望 較2025 年 改 善 ， 且 新 成 長
動 能 將 來 自 於 液 冷 散 熱 相 關 產 品 及 人 形 機 器 人 零 組 件 。 預 估
2026 年 營 收 66.00 億 元 ，YoY+ 32.62 %， 毛 利 率 維 持 高 檔 ， 營 業
利益 YoY+ 97.15 %。 預 估 稅 後 純 益 3.71 億 元 ， YoY+ 148.83 %，
EPS 3.36 元。 隨 著 航 太 、 散 熱 應 用 持 續 發 酵 ， 且 泰 國 新 廠 建 置
完 成 ， 預 估 2027 年 營 收 、 獲 利 動 能 向 上 。

2 群益投顧 
  時碩 (4566  TT) E SG ：  
時碩的 ESG 政 策 由 「 永 續 發 展 暨 風 險 管 理 委 員 會 」 統 籌 ， 並 由 「 風 險 管 理
推 動 委 員 會 」 負 責 執 行 與 追 蹤，將 氣 候 變 遷 及 各 項 永 續 議 題 納 入 公 司 整 體 策
略 與 風 險 管 理 流 程 中。在 環 境 面，公 司 依 循 國 際 標 準 進 行 溫 室 氣 體 盤 查 與 第
三 方 確 信 ， 並 透 過 節 能 設 備 導 入 、 能 源 管 理 與 監 測 系 統 、 製 程 優 化 、 太 陽 能
及綠電等多元措施推動減碳與能源效率提升，同時強化水資源與廢棄物管
理 ， 藉 由 回 收 再 利 用 、 廢 水 循 環 及 資 源 化 設 備 ， 落 實 減 量 及 循 環 經 濟 ； 原 物
料 與 包 材 則 遵 循 RoHS 、REACH 等 規 範，降 低 製 程 對 環 境 的 衝 擊。社 會 面 方
面，時 碩 重 視 員 工 薪 資 福 利、培 訓 與 職 涯 發 展，推 動 性 別 平 等 與 管 理 階 層 多
元 化，並 建 立 職 業 安 全 衛 生 制 度 及 教 育 訓 練，以 降 低 職 災 風 險；在 供 應 鏈 則
透 過 定 期 稽 核 與 評 分 機 制，提 升 供 應 商 在 品 質、交 期 與 服 務 上 的 表 現，並 以
產 品 品 質 與 安 全 為 核 心 ， 確 保 生 產 與 產 品 符 合 相 關 要 求 。 同 時 ， 公 司 以「 企
業 公 民 」 自 我 定 位，投 入 社 會 公 益 與 在 地 關 懷，減 少 營 運 對 社 區 的 負 面 影 響，
並 維 持 完 善 的 資 訊 安 全 管 理 以 避 免 資 訊 外 洩。在 治 理 面，時 碩 建 構 包 含 獨 立
董 事 與 女 性 董 事 的 董 事 會 結 構，設 置 薪 酬 與 審 計 等 功 能 性 委 員 會，訂 定 涵 蓋
營 運 、 財 務、 法 遵 、 環 境 與 氣 候 變 遷 、 人 資 與 人 權 等 面 向 的 風 險 管 理 政 策 ，
並 透 過 法 說 會、網 站 與 永 續 報 告 與 利 害 關 係 人溝 通，同 時 積 極 參 與 各 項 ESG
獎 項 評 選 ， 以 強 化 公 司 治 理 與 永 續 形 象 。

6 群益投顧 
  【 投 資 評 等 說 明 】 
  評等  定義  
  強力買進 (Strong Buy)  首 次 評 等 潛 在 上 漲 空 間≥ 35%  
  買進 (Buy)  15%≤ 首 次 評 等 潛 在 上 漲 空 間<35%  
  區間操作 (Trading Buy)  5%≤ 首 次 評 等 潛 在 上 漲 空 間<15 %

中立 (Neutral)  
無 法 由 基 本 面 給 予 投 資 評 等 
預 期 近 期 股 價 將 處 於 盤 整 
建 議 降 低 持 股

【 投 資 評 等 調 整 機 制 】 
  投 資 評 等 係「 首 次 」給 予 特 定 個 股 投 資 評 等 時，其 前 一 日 收 盤 價 相 較3 個月
目 標 價 之 潛 在 上 漲 空 間 計 算 而 得 。 個 股 投 資 評 等 分 為四 個 等 級 ， 定 義 如 上 。
爾 後 的 投 資 評 等係 依 循 「 首 次 評 等 」 ， 直 到 停 止 推 薦 。  
「 強 力 買 進 」、「 買 進 」及 「 區 間 操 作 」均有 upside 目 標 價 。差 別 僅 在 於 ，
首 次 評 等 時 潛 在 報 酬 率 不 同 。 「 中 立 」 則 無 目 標 價 。 
一 旦 我 們 給 予 特 定 個 股「 強 力 買 進 」、「 買 進 」或「 區 間 操 作 」之 投 資 評 等，
就 是 責 任 的 開 始，爾 後 將 透 過 各 式 研 究 報 告 作 定 期 性、持 續 性 追 蹤 基 本 面 及
股 價 變 化 ， 直 到 停 止 推 薦 。 
停 止 推 薦 情 境 ： 
1.  達 目 標 價 。 
2.  雖 未 達 原 訂 目 標 價 ， 但 檢 視 基 本 面 、 訊 息 面 、 籌 碼 面 等 多 方 訊 息 ， 研
判 股 價 上 漲 空 間 已 然 有 限 ， 將 適 時 出 具 降 評 報 告 。 
3.  推 薦 後 股 價 不 漲 反 跌 ， 亦 將 出 具 降 評 報 告 。

【免責聲明】  
  本 研 究 報 告 僅 提 供 予 特 定 人 之 客 戶 作 為 參 考 資 料「 非 經 同 意 不 得 轉 載 」 。我
們 並 不 確 保 此 資 訊 的 完 整 性 與 正 確 性，投 資 人 應 了 解，報 告 中 有 關 未 來 預 測
之 陳 述 可 能 不 會 實 現，因 而 不 應 被 依 賴。而 且 此 報 告 並 非 根 據 特 定 投 資 目 的
或 依 預 定 對 象 之 財 務 狀 況 所 撰 寫 出 來 的，因 此，此 研 究 報 告 的 目 的，既 非 對
投 資 人 於 買 賣 證 券、選 擇 權、期 貨 或 其 他 證 券 相 關 之 衍 生 性 商 品 提 供 詢 價 服
務，亦 非 作 為 進 行 交 易 的 要 約。投 資 人 應 注 意 到 相 關 證 券 之 價 值 及 收 益，可
能會有無預警地上升或下降，產生投資回報金額可能比原始投資來得少的
情形。

### Page 2

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
2
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
ECONOMIC INSIGHT
• Recent revisions to US PCE inflation, UK productivity
and Canadian population estimates are reminders that
statistical measurement is imperfect. Revisions are both a
fact of life and, when they improve measurement, desirable.
• Australian data will also be revised over time, though

not necessarily in the same ways as these latest revisions
abroad. The ABS tends to favour quality over speed,
which reduces the need for early revisions as more data
become available. But revisions can still matter. Population
estimates implied in the initial releases of labour market and
GDP data are especially likely to be revised in the near term.
• Estimates of current trends can change as future data are
published. Shifting seasonal patterns are another source

of revision, especially for monthly CPI components with
short histories. Series that are derived from multiple other
imperfectly measured series are more prone to revision
than quantities that can be measured directly. All these
considerations point to a need for humility when using
recent data to make strong policy pronouncements.
On September 30, the Fed’s preferred measure of inflation,
PCE, was revised down. New methods and data sources were
applied to three components of services inflation (financial

advice, software and legal services), large enough to result in
downward revisions to the overall read on core PCE inflation.
These changes had been pre-announced in June and were
therefore not a surprise. The CPI measure of inflation was not
affected, nor should the revisions materially change your view
of US inflationary pressures.
Some revisions are more consequential for interpretation and
policy. A couple of weeks before the US PCE revision, it was

revealed that the UK Office for National Statistics was revising
down its estimates of average hours worked per worker. The
result was that measured productivity growth was nowhere
near as weak as had been widely believed. As my friend and
counterpart, TS Lombard’s Chief Economist Freya Beamish
commented on social media, “Oh well. Not like any major policy
decisions were taken on the basis of those numbers.” Quite.
Also in September, Statistics Canada revised up its estimates

of Canada’s population, taking the recent growth rates
from outright contraction to still-slow growth a little below
½%yr. The number of temporary residents in Canada is still
falling, but the revised data do a better job of tracking them,
especially those seeking an extension on their permit. (An alert
When reality gets revised
Luci Ellis
Chief Economist, Westpac Group
for customers who have seen the cross-country population
graph I often use at client events: the Canadian line now looks

different but still shows a sharp slowing.)
There is no fault to be attributed here. Revisions to key
statistics are a part of economic life. Sometimes the underlying
data arrive with a lag, perhaps because of reporting deadlines.
Sometimes the compiling agency improves its methods or
gains access to a better data source. The US and UK examples
above fall into this category. In the Canadian case, better
data and a more accurate definition of scope were involved.

All these revisions should be welcomed. They provide a more
accurate picture of the underlying reality.
Australia, revised
Revisions are a fact of life for Australian statistics, too. I am
still a little scarred by that unexpected –0.5%qtr GDP print
when the September quarter 2016 data were first released on
my first day as RBA Assistant Governor; the figure has since
been revised to 0%qtr and was followed by a big bounce-back
in the December quarter.

The recent revisions mentioned above are less likely to be
replicated in Australia, though. Australia’s CPI is designed such
that the headline (not seasonally adjusted) measure is never
revised. This is because it is used in many legal contracts.
If an error is detected, the ABS makes the adjustment in
subsequent months and quarters rather than revising the
history. Our labour force survey has a much higher response
rate than its UK equivalent – Australians are a compliant lot

when the ABS comes knocking – and measures actual hours
worked more reliably. And our population statistics already
handle the array of temporary resident visas with the Bureau
instead applying a standard 12-month-in-16-month residency
test to count as a resident for the population statistics rather
than using a visa-classification basis.
This last case illustrates Australia’s broader tendency to
favour quality over speed in official statistics. Our CPI and

national accounts data tend to be published a bit later than
peer countries’ data for the same reference period, and our
“Economic data are often revised. Sometimes
the revisions change the story; sometimes
they do not."

### Page 3

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
3
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
estimated resident population data is quite a bit lagged
relative to Canada’s (the 12-month-in-16 criteria taking over
a year to confirm). The trade-off is that observers of the
Australian economy must wait longer but can be less worried
about “flash” and “advance” releases of data that can be

quickly revised to tell a completely different story.
Revisions to data still can matter in Australia, and it helps
to be aware of the specific cases where it might occur. For
example, precisely because the population data are very
lagged, the population estimates underlying the initial
releases of the labour force survey and national accounts are
based on preliminary data and get revised later as a matter
of routine. This is why ratios such as the unemployment,

employment-to-population and participation rates provide a
better real-time gauge of labour market conditions than the
monthly change in employment. Similarly, GDP per capita can
be revised even when GDP is not. This type of revision is also
a more likely source of short-term changes in measured hours
worked in the Australian productivity data than the conceptual
change that shifted the UK data.
The usual reporting lags – and occasional correction of

reporting errors by the reporting entity – will always be a factor
in Australia just as it is in other countries. The annual national
accounts, due on 23 October, incorporate additional data, revise
the quarterly estimates and provide first estimates of important
quantities such as the capital stock. Just as importantly, though,
the annual accounts reconcile the different ways of measuring
GDP. In doing so, what we thought we knew about the recent

history of output growth can change.
Every purchase is also a sale, so conceptually, you should get the
same answer for final output regardless of whether you count
how much is produced, how much people spent on it, or how
much income people got for producing it. In practice, though,
the “production”, “expenditure” and “income” approaches do
not always line up until the ABS goes through a more thorough
reconciliation process in compiling the annual accounts.

When the future changes the past
Future data can also change our view of the past. Two good
examples are trend estimation and seasonal adjustment. Future
observations help reveal whether an apparent trend is stable.
As a result, real-time trend estimates can differ considerably
from those calculated with the benefit of later data.
Identifying stable seasonal patterns also requires a reasonable
run of data. Future observations affect whether an earlier

movement is judged to be normal seasonal variation. This
problem is more pronounced for newer series, where there is
not yet enough history to establish what “normal” looks like.
Consider, then, the monthly CPI. This relatively new measure has
only a short run of data with many individual components having
too little history to use standard seasonal adjustment methods.
As Westpac’s senior economist Justin Smirk has previously
highlighted, some components such as footwear are being

‘seasonally adjusted’ by applying Henderson trends, similar
to those used in the ‘trend’ measures of labour market and
national accounts data. For some other components, including
books, furniture, audio-visual and computing equipment and
children’s clothes, certain price falls – usually associated with
sales periods – are just arbitrarily removed from the seasonally
adjusted series. The index is held at its previous month’s level
but otherwise matches the non-seasonally adjusted series.

The seasonally adjusted measures of these CPI components will
inevitably be revised over time, and thus so could the monthly
trimmed mean estimates. This helps explain why the RBA has
said it would, for now, focus on the quarterly trimmed mean
based on the old seasonal factors. That measure will at least
not be buffeted by shifting measurement of seasonality in a
short history. If seasonal patterns are themselves also shifting,
the current underlying trend might look a bit different in a few

years, not just a smoother month-to-month profile.
The broader point here is to remember that economic statistics
are a lens on reality. Sometimes the lens is smudged, sometimes
the scope is off and sometimes what is being read as underlying
trends is wrong. Measures constructed from several imperfect
series – productivity from output and hours worked, or unit labour
costs from productivity and labour costs, or savings rates derived
from income and spending – are especially prone to revision

compared with concepts that can be measured more directly, like
a price. That is a strong argument for humility before making firm
policy pronouncements based on simple data comparisons.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
THE WEEK THAT WAS
Cliff Notes: mulling the minutes
consistent with a labour market that is gradually normalising
than one entering a meaningful downturn.
The September FOMC minutes reinforced a distinctly hawkish
message, with participants noting they had "not seen

sufficient progress on lowering inflation in recent months" and
expressing concern that inflation risks remained "skewed to the
upside". Policymakers highlighted that both core services and
core goods inflation remained elevated, with some attributing
persistent goods price strength to the effects of the AI buildout
despite the sector's traditional disinflationary role. There was
also concern that, after more than five years of inflation above
target, elevated inflation could begin to influence inflation

expectations and wage- and price-setting behaviour. In
contrast, labour market conditions were generally viewed as
stable and "close to maximum employment". While a majority
noted the labour market had strengthened somewhat recently,
several participants highlighted unusually low dynamism,
characterised by low hiring, low layoffs, a low job-finding
rate and elevated long-term unemployment. Importantly,
participants generally judged that aggregate wage growth

was moderate and "consistent with inflation moving toward 2
percent", with several explicitly noting that "the labor market
was not currently a source of inflationary pressures".
While participants broadly expected AI-related investment to
boost productivity growth and potential output over time, several
warned that "the AI buildout could cause aggregate demand
to outpace aggregate supply over the medium term, putting
upward pressure on inflation". Consistent with this view, some

participants had raised their estimate of the neutral policy rate
and several judged the current policy stance to be "not restrictive
or only mildly restrictive". Against this backdrop, all participants
supported the September rate increase, and most assessed
that another increase in the federal funds rate would likely be
appropriate by year-end, although future decisions would remain
data dependent.
The September ISM Services PMI released this week painted

a similar picture of an economy that remains resilient but is
gradually losing momentum. The headline index eased to 54.9
from 55.4 in August, with the decline driven by a sharp fall in
business activity and a 9.4pt drop in new export orders, which
slipped into contractionary territory for the first time in eight
The only data release of note in Australia this week was
the October Westpac-MI Consumer Sentiment Survey ,
which reported a 4.7% drop in the headline index. At

80.4, sentiment has tumbled back to deeply pessimistic
levels, similar to the post-pandemic cost-of-living shock
when readings were among the weakest in the survey’s
more than fifty-year history. Indeed, inflation and interest
rate pressures are once again clearly front of mind for
consumers, with both the view on ‘family finances vs a
year ago’ and ‘family finance next 12 months’ falling 8.0%
and 6.4% in the month, respectively. Moreover, the RBA’s

September rate hike looks to have had a significant impact –
sentiment tanked 20% between those surveyed before the
RBA decision and those surveyed afterwards.
Views on the economic outlook were relatively more resilient,
but they still weakened, with the one-year and five-year ahead
sub-indexes falling 1.6% and 0.4% respectively. Consumers
are becoming more ‘on edge’ about job security, albeit not
outright alarmed. The unemployment rate remains relatively

low versus history, although it has drifted up to a cycle high
of 4.6% as labour supply continues to outstrip demand. We
expect it to hold there in next week’s September Labour Force
Survey – see our preview for more detail. All told, with higher
interest rates, renewed inflation pressures and a softening
jobs market all weighing at once, households are entering the
final months of the year on the back foot.
Offshore, the focus was on US data.
Before turning to this week's releases, it is worth noting that

September non-farm payrolls came in softer than expected
across most metrics. Employment rose by only 29k (89k
expected), and there were sizeable downward revisions
to prior months, with a combined 60k removed from July
and August payrolls, leaving gains of just 31k and 29k
respectively. The details pointed to weaker hiring in leisure
& hospitality and education & health services industries,
although construction and manufacturing employment
continued to benefit from ongoing data-centre-related

investment. The unemployment rate edged up to 4.2%, with
softer employment occurring alongside a rise in labour force
participation to 61.8%. Meanwhile wage growth moderated
to 0.1%mth (3.0%yr). Taken together, the report suggests
labour demand is cooling, with the three-month average
pace of job creation slowing to around 50k. However, softer
payroll gains are not necessarily indicative of labour market
deterioration. Lower immigration and ageing demographics

have reduced the pace of employment growth needed to keep
the unemployment rate stable. As such, the report is more
Sian Fenner, Head of Business and Industry Economics

AI investment, productive capacity and
inflation."

months. Labour market conditions improved modestly with

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
FOCUS ON NEW ZEALAND
Fuel, housing and the bumpy road ahead
Higher fuel costs, a weaker exchange rate, and a softer
housing market have prompted a refresh of our near-term
forecasts. Inflation looks set to finish 2026 above 4%, while
growth could lose a little momentum into year-end. Even so,

we continue to expect the RBNZ to pause in October before
lifting the OCR to 3% in December.
New Zealand’s recovery is still moving forward, but the road
has become rougher. Since our last forecast update in August,
global oil and refined-fuel prices have climbed sharply, the
New Zealand dollar has fallen, and longer-term interest rates
have moved higher. At home, the housing market has remained
weaker than we anticipated. None of those developments,

on their own, are enough to derail the recovery. Together,
however, they point to a tougher finish to 2026 for households
and businesses. We have therefore revisited our near-term
inflation, housing and growth forecasts. The broad story
remains one of continued economic expansion and gradually
improving labour demand, but with a more pronounced
inflation spike and slightly less momentum in consumer
spending. Our OCR call is unchanged: a pause at the October

review, followed by a 25 basis point increase to 3% at the
December Monetary Policy Statement.
A temporary inflation reprieve
We continue to think that consumer prices rose 0.7% in the
September quarter. That would lower annual inflation from
4.1% in June to 3.7% in September, broadly consistent with
the monthly price updates published so far. The final monthly
release for the quarter is due on 16 October and will help firm up
that estimate. But any easing in the annual rate is likely to prove

short-lived. We now expect prices to rise 1.1% in the December
quarter, lifting annual inflation to a three-year high of 4.2% by
year-end. That is half a percentage point above our previous
projection and above the RBNZ’s September forecast of 3.9%.
The reason is easy to see at the petrol pump. Higher global
fuel prices have been amplified by a more than 4% fall in
the New Zealand dollar since the start of September. Local
petrol prices have risen by around 50 cents a litre over the

past month, with the nationwide average price of 91 unleaded
reaching a record $3.55 a litre this week. RBNZ Governor
Breman acknowledged a couple of weeks ago that higher
oil prices have tilted the risks around the near-term inflation
outlook to the upside. Importantly, however, the RBNZ remains
focused on the medium-term inflation outlook rather than
trying to offset every movement in volatile fuel prices.
For now, we have left our quarterly inflation forecasts for 2027

unchanged. Even so, the higher starting point at the end of this
year means annual inflation is likely to remain firm through much
of 2027. Our updated profile has inflation at 3.7% in March
2027, 2.6% in June, 2.5% in September and 1.7% in December.
That is broadly similar to the RBNZ’s path through the first three
quarters, although our forecast falls more quickly late in the
year. We will take a fuller look at the 2027 inflation and growth
outlook next week alongside the monthly Market Outlook.

Housing fails to find its feet
The housing market has also delivered a disappointment. We
had expected prices to stabilise through the second half of
2026, but sales volumes have continued to weaken, and the
median time required to sell a property has lengthened to 48
days. Average prices have kept edging lower, despite firmer
conditions in parts of the South Island with greater exposure
to the strong export economy. The latest indicators do not
suggest that a decisive turn is close. We have therefore revised

our forecast for national house price growth in 2026 from a
0.6% rise to a 1.4% decline. That incorporates quarterly falls
of 0.5% in September and 0.8% in December.
The headwinds are familiar: the labour market remains soft,
mortgage rates are higher and employment growth is still
moderate. Those forces are likely to keep the market subdued
for some time, with risks tilted towards another weak year
in 2027. Changes to the taxation of capital gains on investor

housing following the 2026 General Election may add another
potential drag.
Kelly Eckhold
Chief Economist NZ

### Page 7

the forecasts may be affected by incorrect assumptions or by known or unknown risks and uncertainties. The results ultimately achieved may differ substantially from these forecasts.
7
WESTPAC ECONOMICS
Regional outcomes will continue to differ. Tourism- and
agriculture-focused areas should benefit from stronger income
and employment growth, supported by robust commodity export
prices. We have lifted our 2026/27 Fonterra Milk Price forecast to
$9.75/kg and set an opening forecast of $9.50/kg for 2027/28. In

contrast, homeowners in the major North Island cities may have to
wait longer for the wealth boost that comes with rising property
values. Data released this week shows that household wealth
continues to be dragged down by declining housing values.
A little less momentum
The weaker housing backdrop matters for the wider economy.
When petrol absorbs more of the household budget and home
values are drifting lower, consumers tend to become more
cautious. That combination is likely to crimp discretionary

spending in the run-up to Christmas and could take some
steam out of the construction recovery. That’s even though our
own card spending data for September  showed resilience in
consumer spending. Recent business and consumer confidence
surveys have nevertheless remained resilient despite the rise
in energy prices and long-term interest rates. Our short-term
growth forecasts therefore remain broadly intact, but we have
trimmed our December quarter consumer spending projection

and now expect GDP growth of 2.5% over 2026. That is still
a solid result, although the balance of risks has shifted to
the downside.
There are several moving parts to watch. Sustained high fuel
prices would squeeze household purchasing power more than
we have allowed for. The sharp lift in long-term interest rates
may also weigh on activity through 2027, even if shorter-
term borrowing costs evolve as expected. The election result
introduces two-sided risks that are difficult to quantify before

the shape and timing of policy changes are known. Against that,
strong agricultural incomes and signs of improving employment
growth should provide a buffer. We have not changed our 2026
labour market forecasts. Hiring appears to be strengthening
gradually, but it will take time before that improvement
translates into a noticeable fall in the unemployment rate.
The RBNZ should still pause before Christmas
Despite the higher near-term inflation track, we are not

changing our OCR forecast. Since the July Monetary Policy
Review, our view has been that the RBNZ would slow the pace
of increases as the OCR approached the Bank’s estimate of a
neutral 3%. The September meeting clearly signalled a pause
in October and one further rise in December. We continue to
regard that as the most likely path. The latest developments
raise headline inflation but, so far, there is little evidence that
inflation expectations are moving higher or that the fuel shock

is generating broader, persistent price pressure. At the same
time, growth risks warrant some caution.
A pause in October would give the Monetary Policy Committee
time to assess the cumulative effect of the increases already
delivered and to refine its strategy for 2027. Financial
conditions have also tightened from an unexpected source:
longer-term interest rates have risen markedly over the past
six weeks. That increase has at least offset the easing impulse

from the lower exchange rate, even though the weaker
currency is adding to imported inflation in the near term. We
still think the RBNZ is likely to have more work to do in 2027,
but there is plenty of data to come before that judgement
needs to be made.
For now, the best description of the outlook is resilient but
less comfortable. Export incomes remain strong, activity is
expanding and the labour market is beginning to improve. But
households face an awkward combination of record petrol

prices, soft home values and higher borrowing costs. That
mix points to a bumpy end to the year: inflation will be hotter,
spending growth a little cooler and the recovery more uneven
across regions. The RBNZ can afford to wait in October, but if
the economy develops broadly as we expect, a further hike to
3% in December will still be needed.

AUS: Sep Labour Force – Employment Change (000s)
Oct 15, Last: 39.5, Westpac f/c: 20 
Market f/c: 20, Range: flat to 35
Employment surprised to the upside in August with a 39k gain. 
Looking through the monthly swings, while momentum has 
improved over recent months, employment growth has largely 
continued to bump around below its historic average and the 
current pace of working age population growth.
Our preview provides a deeper look into the various gauges 
of labour demand – the LFS suggests employment growth 
is sluggish but broader measures in the Q2 Labour Account 
showed a stronger narrative. Meanwhile job ads and business 
surveys are also pulling in different directions.
For September, we have pencilled in a lift in employment of 
+20k, which would see annual growth hold in recent ranges.
AUS: Sep Labour Force – Unemployment Rate (%)
Oct 15, Last: 4.6, Westpac f/c: 4.6 
Market f/c: 4.6, Range: 4.5 to 4.7
The participation rate jumped to 67.1% in August, just under 
the 115-year high recorded in January 2025. With labour 
supply once again outstripping demand, the unemployment 
rate rose from 4.5% to 4.6% – the highest since in late-2021, 
close to rounding up to 4.7% (4.646% at three decimal places).
A softer economy and sub-par employment growth typically 
discourages some people to search for work, but cost-of-living 
pressures and interest rate rises are more than outweighing this 
effect, encouraging more people into the labour market.
With the participation rate expected to pare back to 67.0%, we 
think the unemployment rate will hold steady at 4.6%. Please 
see our preview for more detail.

WESTPAC ECONOMICS
NZ: Sep REINZ House Sales and Prices (%ann)
Oct 14, Sales Last: –13.0, Prices Last: –0.9
The housing market has remained soft in recent months,
with sales falling to a two-year low and prices edging lower.
Mortgage rates have risen as the RBNZ has initiated a new

tightening cycle, and a growing supply of unsold homes on the
market means that buyers are under no pressure to bid prices
higher. Conditions remain stronger in the south, with prices
up on last year in Canterbury, Otago and Southland, while
Auckland and Wellington are the weakest markets.
NZ: Sep Retail Card Spending (%mth)
Oct 16, Last: –0.9, Westpac f/c: 0.8
We’re forecasting a 0.8% rise in retail spending in September,
reversing the 0.9% fall seen in the previous month. In part, the

forecast rise in spending is due to the sharp increases in petrol
prices in recent weeks. Those higher fuel costs are weighing on
spending in other categories like hospitality. Nevertheless, we
still expect a 0.5% rise in core (ex-fuel) spending. Stats NZ’s
monthly retail spending figures have been very choppy for the
past two years, and we’re likely to see a bounce following last
month’s unexpected falls in categories like furnishings.
NZ: Sep Selected Consumer Prices (%mth)

Oct 16
This will be the last monthly update ahead of the full quarterly
CPI release on 22 October. We’re currently forecasting a 0.7%
quarterly rise in the CPI (+3.7%yr) and will be watching the
detail of the Selected Prices update to pin down that forecast.
In terms of the big categories, we’re forecasting a roughly 6%
rise in petrol costs, along with a 0.7% rise in food prices. That’s
balanced against continued softness in housing rents (which
are the single largest component of the CPI). The key sources

of risk for our forecast are the volatile travel categories, most
of which recorded sizeable drops last month that we expect to
partially reverse in September.

CHIEF INVESTMENT OFFICE
Capital Market Outlook

### Page 2

2 of 8 September 8, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
MACRO STRATEGY
Commodities: From Inflation Hedge to Structural Growth Asset
Anna Snider, Managing Director and Head of Investment Manager Selection
(Due Diligence)
A broader role in portfolios: For decades, commodities occupied a relatively narrow
role in portfolios: protection against inflation when rising prices eroded the value
of traditional assets. That function remains important, but the investment case is

broadening. Commodities now sit at the intersection of geopolitical fragmentation,
supply-chain security, reshoring, inventory rebuilding, and the buildout of AI and digital
infrastructure. Together, these forces are turning parts of the asset class from purely
cyclical exposures into potential long-term growth beneficiaries.
The diversification case is also becoming more relevant. In Q1, when inflation concerns
peaked, the Bloomberg Commodity Index returned close to 25%, outperforming both

Equities and Fixed Income. At the same time, the relationship among major asset
classes has shifted. Equity and Fixed Income correlations have tightened in recent years,
while commodities have moved in the opposite direction: over the trailing five years,
their correlation with both Equities and bonds has been negative. That combination of
inflation sensitivity and differentiated return behavior can make commodities useful
beyond a simple tactical hedge.

AI infrastructure is a physical buildout: AI is often viewed through semiconductors,
software platforms, and hyperscale data centers. Yet the infrastructure required to
deploy AI is extraordinarily commodity intensive. It requires continuous electricity,
generation capacity, transmission lines, substations, transformers, cooling systems,
networking hardware and structural materials. In that sense, AI is not only a computing
revolution. It is also an energy, infrastructure, and critical-materials investment cycle.

Power is the first constraint. Data centers require reliable, around-the-clock electricity.
Natural gas remains a practical source of incremental generation in the U.S., while
nuclear power is emerging as a strategic long-term option for hyperscalers seeking
stable baseload supply. Solar and wind can add capacity and support decarbonization
goals, but grid reliability and storage become more important as power demand rises.
That supports investment in batteries, backup power and grid-scale storage, with lithium

and graphite among the relevant inputs.
The buildout also increases demand for industrial metals. Copper is a clear beneficiary
because it is essential to transmission, transformers, cooling systems and data-center
wiring. Aluminum and iron ore are used in facilities, electrical equipment, transmission
networks and supporting infrastructure. Semiconductor fabrication adds another layer
of demand through industrial gases such as helium and neon, high-purity quartz for

wafers and optics, and gallium and germanium for advanced chips, photonics, and
telecommunications. AI-enabled automation and robotics also depend on permanent
magnets that use rare earth elements, including neodymium-praseodymium and
dysprosium-terbium.
Demand has moved faster than supply: This demand surge resembles earlier cycles
in semiconductors and memory, where expectations pulled spending forward. Metals
and mining companies are already seeing earnings growth and margin expansion,

yet the industry has struggled to keep pace with demand. The imbalance matters
because commodity supply is slow to respond. Production of many strategic materials
is geographically concentrated, permitting timelines are lengthy, recycling capacity is
limited, and substitution can remain years from commercial viability. New mines and
processing facilities can take a decade or more to reach production.
Investment Implications
Commodities may increasingly serve

three portfolio purposes: inflation
protection, diversification from
traditional assets, and participation in
long-term infrastructure and resource-
demand growth, supporting a diversified
allocation across direct commodities
and select producer Equities rather than
a single directional trade.

### Page 3

3 of 8 September 8, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
Governments are also taking a larger role through industrial policy, export controls,
stockpiling and efforts to secure critical supply chains. Restrictions involving gallium,
germanium, graphite and other strategic materials underscore how geopolitics can affect
availability and pricing. As a result, the current cycle may produce greater scarcity and
pricing power than demand growth alone would suggest.

So far, this dynamic has been most visible in industrial metals, but the same forces could
extend into agriculture. Reshoring changes where food and inputs are produced and
processed, while inventory rebuilding can lift demand for fertilizer, fuel, machinery and
storage. Agriculture has not yet experienced the same earnings and pricing impact as
metals, but supply-chain resilience could make it a broader beneficiary over time.
Cyclical forces still matter: The structural thesis does not eliminate near-term

volatility. Energy remains a key transmission mechanism for inflation because oil and
natural gas underpin transportation, manufacturing and industrial production. Geopolitical
disruptions can cascade through supply chains, raising costs and moving both commodity
futures and spot prices. Currency dynamics matter as well. A stronger U.S. dollar can
restrain commodity prices by increasing costs for non-U.S. buyers, while renewed dollar
weakness could support emerging-market demand and reprice dollar-denominated

assets.
A stabilization of geopolitical tensions could create a more constructive cyclical backdrop
through lower energy volatility, improved business confidence and stronger capital
investment. Conversely, renewed conflict can produce sharp and uneven moves across
the complex. These crosscurrents reinforce the value of treating commodities as a
diversified allocation rather than a single directional trade.
Gold, implementation and the portfolio case: Gold is fundamentally different from

most industrial commodities, but it deserves attention. Its traditional relationships with
interest rates and the dollar have been less reliable lately. Even so, sustained central bank
and exchange-traded fund buying points to durable long-term demand despite bouts of
volatility such as the selloff earlier this year. Gold also tended to move inversely with oil
during the height of the Iran conflict, illustrating how different commodities can respond

differently to the same geopolitical shock.
Investors can access the theme through commodity futures or producer Equities. Direct
commodity exposure is generally the clearer vehicle for inflation protection, scarcity, and
geopolitical supply shocks. Producer equities can offer greater upside when commodity
prices rise faster than operating costs because earnings and margins expand, but that
leverage is asymmetric. Company-specific execution, financing, cost and valuation risks

can still cause Equities to underperform the underlying commodity. A blended approach
may capture both physical scarcity and corporate operating leverage.
Ultimately, commodities remain sensitive to energy markets, currencies, growth, and
geopolitics, but their portfolio role is expanding. They can provide inflation protection and
diversification while offering exposure to AI infrastructure, electrification, energy security,
defense modernization, reshoring and, potentially, agricultural supply-chain investment.

The strongest beneficiaries of the AI trade may therefore extend well beyond technology
companies to the materials and power systems that make deployment possible.

### Page 4

4 of 8 September 8, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
MARKET VIEW
Rising Global Interest Rates and International Markets
Ehiwario Efeyini, Director and Senior Investment Strategist
Strong earnings growth has so far delivered a fourth consecutive year of double-digit
gains for global Equity indexes in 2026, propelling them to new all-time highs. But a
growing risk throughout the year has been the increase in government bond yields, which

have also broken out to multi-decade highs across a number of the major markets. The
U.S. Treasury will this week begin its expanded Treasury securities buyback program
announced in mid-August in response to rising rates. But 10-year yields across all
the major G10 countries have moved higher, in many cases by as much as or more
than in the U.S. market. Against a backdrop of above-trend growth in nominal gross
domestic product (GDP), high energy prices and elevated inflation, investors appear to

be reassessing monetary policy expectations, particularly in Europe where most of the
2026 increase in 10-year yields has been driven by inflation breakevens (Exhibit 1). Fixed
Income markets are consequently looking for policy rate hikes from the European Central
Bank later this week and potentially from the Bank of Japan later next month.
The rise in global interest rates has also come alongside an expansion in fiscal policy
across the major markets. In Europe, 2026 and 2027 are expected to be the peak

spending years for defense and infrastructure expenditure plans unveiled last year. The
so-called Readiness 2030 package allocates €800 billion to increased European Union
defense spending as it looks to move toward its 5% of GDP target by 2035. And at the
same time, the German government relaxation of its national debt brake has directed
€500 billion toward funding for investment in clean energy, transportation and digital
infrastructure. Similarly in Japan, the government supermajority is looking to enact a

new stimulus aimed at raising investment levels across 17 strategic sectors such as
semiconductors, defense and shipbuilding, in addition to a planned two-year cut to the
consumption tax on food from April 2027. And in the UK, the new central government
leadership is also likely to announce new spending measures when its first national
budget is released next month. To the extent that increased public investment spending
can lift productivity and support nominal GDP growth in Europe and Japan, the rise in

yields need not undermine economic growth or derail the equity market advance. But
higher rates, especially in response to supply-driven energy price inflation, nonetheless
remain a risk for developed market equity returns going into the end of the year.
Exhibit 1: Global Bond Yields in Major Markets have Risen by as Much or More
than U.S. Yields in 2026.

For many emerging markets the picture looks quite different. In contrast with the U.S.,
Japan and Europe, inflation rates are generally well below central bank targets outside
a limited number of resource exporters in Latin America and the booming AI-linked
economies of Korea and Taiwan (Exhibit 2). As a result, policy rate expectations over

the coming quarters are stable or lower for several emerging markets (including China,
Malaysia, Thailand, Poland, Brazil and Mexico) according to Bloomberg consensus forecasts.
Investment Implications
Ongoing strength in economic growth
and corporate earnings should support
global equity markets even in the face
of rising rates. For Emerging Markets
(EM) Equities in particular, contained
inflation, flat to lower local policy rate
expectations and stronger fundamentals

should limit the impact. And we
continue to favor EM Equities over
International Developed Markets, where
inflation levels are generally still above
target, central banks are expected
to raise rates and investors will be
watching for fiscal sustainability as
governments implement expansionary
budget plans.

Historically, rising U.S. interest rates have been a major headwind for emerging markets.
But looking ahead in the current cycle, we still expect the impact of potential Federal
Reserve (Fed) rate hikes to be lower than in the past. Over recent years, the association
between tighter Fed policy and weaker EM returns appears to have diminished. EM
Equities for example were resilient in the face of aggressive U.S. rate increases in 2022
and 2023, broadly keeping pace with the rest of the world. This is in sharp contrast to

prior cycles in which tighter Fed policy has resulted in capital flight, deleveraging and
falling asset values across the emerging world. Particularly during the crisis periods of
the 1980s and 1990s—but also more recently during the 2015 taper tantrum—U.S.
monetary tightening has tended to attract capital away from riskier emerging countries
(particularly those with large current account deficits), reducing support for local asset
prices and exchange rates.

Associated strength in the U.S. dollar has typically been an additional hurdle. For those
emerging economies with fixed exchange rates, a loss of export competitiveness has
further undermined current account positions, forcing central banks to run down their
foreign exchange reserves. And where exchange rates are floating, foreign currency
debts have risen in local currency terms, resulting in widespread defaults, debt
downgrades, recessions and additional exchange rate depreciation.

But starting in the years that followed the 1997-1998 Asian financial crisis and
continuing into the current cycle, the fundamentals across much of the EM universe have
improved. Fixed exchange rates have been abandoned. EM foreign currency reserves have
roughly doubled as a share of GDP . The share of EM debt denominated in U.S. dollars
has fallen significantly, led by the private sector across households and corporates. And
for most countries, current account deficits have narrowed or disappeared entirely. As a

result, emerging markets have become much more insulated from the direction of U.S.
interest rates on a structural basis, and this has only been reinforced by the AI-driven
revaluation of key Asian markets in 2026. Greater concentration in Korea and Taiwan has
increased the EM weighting in the surplus Asia-Pacific region to 83% as at the end of
August—up from 81% at the start of the year and 71% a decade ago.
Above-trend growth in nominal GDP and ongoing strength in corporate earnings should

support global equity markets even in the face of rising interest rates. But for emerging
Equities in particular, contained inflation relative to central bank targets, flat to lower
local policy rate expectations and stronger economic fundamentals should limit the
impact from higher global rates. We continue to favor EM Equities over International
Developed Markets, where inflation levels are generally still above target, central banks
are expected to raise interest rates and investors will be watching for fiscal sustainability

as governments implement expansionary budget plans.

### Page 6

6 of 8 September 8, 2026 – Capital Market Outlook RETURN TO FIRST PAGE
THOUGHT OF THE WEEK
The One-Offs Inflating Q2 Earnings
Lauren Sanfilippo, Director and Senior Investment Strategist
Headline Q2 earnings numbers look almost too good to be true. And in a sense, they are.
With nearly all of S&P 500 companies’ results reported, EPS growth is tracking around
50% year-over-year (YoY)—not just an acceleration from Q1 but a second consecutive
quarter above 20%. Chiefly skewing the headline number are massive mark-to-market

gains on private investments for a handful of mega cap technology companies like
Alphabet and Amazon that booked outsized non-operating gains tied to their stakes in
Anthropic, while Microsoft benefited from its OpenAI investment. Strip those out, and
adjusted EPS growth notches down to 31%. The point being, after being sliced and
diced, 30%+ is well above any long-run historical earnings growth rate.
The one-off nature of earnings strength wasn’t limited to investment gains. A second,

less-discussed boost was tariff refunds. As Exhibit 3 illustrates, revenues from tariffs
steadily climbed from early 2025 through mid-2026 until the International Emergency
Economic Powers Act (IEEPA) tariff ruling which initiated the refund process. As
refunds were disbursed, customs receipts turned net negative in June and July, with
refund payments exceeding total tariff collections. Customs and Border Protection cash
withdrawals of more than $100 billion in refunds have now been paid—60% of the $165

billion in total eligible claims.
The recipients: the “importers of record,” or the dozens of S&P 500 companies that
have disclosed billions in refunds over the past two quarters; Apple alone received $2.19
billion, adding $0.11 to per-share earnings to their quarter. Other disclosed refunds
include Nike with $986 million, Amazon with $640 million, and General Motors refunded
$500 million (although accounting treatment and use of proceeds has varied).

The boost of investment gains and/or refunds may not be repeatable in future quarters.
Perhaps more telling is that the median company’s revenue grew roughly 15% YoY, the
strongest gain in years, corroborating that there is operating momentum rather than
just an accounting mirage for Q2 earnings. Margins, too, as a percent of GDP are at
their highest back to 1950, hovering north of 22%, see Exhibit 4. That this is occurring
amid rising rates and at a time of geopolitical uncertainty/disruption, rather than a post-

recessionary recovery, makes the results that much more impressive.
The Path Forward: Questions remain about the sustainability of these elevated growth
rates, although consensus expects EPS growth to stay above 20% in both Q3 and Q4.
If achieved, every quarter this year would see 20%-plus EPS growth. On the back of
brighter outlooks from Corporate America in recent weeks, full-year earnings growth
will decelerate into the mid-teens in 2027, but that moderation comes off a high base

of 2026. For now, the breadth of earnings growth is the encouraging story as 10 of
the 11 sectors reported growth (Energy and Communication Services leading the way,
Healthcare the only detractor). That’s a reflection of strong core businesses growing
revenues into 2027’s tougher YoY comparisons.
Investment Implications
Underlying earnings-per-share (EPS)
growth of around 30%, stripped
of investment gains, is historically
exceptional. It remains that robust

corporate earnings and positive
revisions have helped Equities absorb
concerns around the recent rise
in yields.

Investments have varying degrees of risk. Some of the risks involved with equity securities include the possibility that the value of the stocks may fluctuate in response to events specific to

the companies or markets, as well as economic, political or social events in the U.S. or abroad. Small cap and mid cap companies pose special risks, including possible illiquidity and greater price
volatility than funds consisting of larger, more established companies. Investing in fixed-income securities may involve certain risks, including the credit quality of individual issuers, possible
prepayments, market or economic developments and yields and share price fluctuations due to changes in interest rates. When interest rates go up, bond prices typically drop, and vice versa.

Investments in high-yield bonds (sometimes referred to as “junk bonds”) offer the potential for high current income and attractive total return, but involves certain risks. Changes in economic
conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect a junk bond issuer’s ability to make principal and interest payments. Income from investing in municipal bonds is generally exempt
from Federal and state taxes for residents of the issuing state. While the interest income is tax-exempt, any capital gains distributed are taxable to the investor. Income for some investors

may be subject to the Federal Alternative Minimum Tax (AMT). Treasury bills are less volatile than longer-term fixed income securities and are guaranteed as to timely payment of principal and
interest by the U.S. government. Bonds are subject to interest rate, inflation and credit risks. Investments in foreign securities (including ADRs) involve special risks, including foreign currency
risk and the possibility of substantial volatility due to adverse political, economic or other developments. These risks are magnified for investments made in emerging markets. Investments in

a certain industry or sector may pose additional risk due to lack of diversification and sector concentration. There are special risks associated with an investment in commodities, such as gold,
including market price fluctuations, regulatory changes, interest rate changes, credit risk, economic changes and the impact of adverse political or financial factors.
Alternative investments are speculative and involve a high degree of risk.
Alternative investments are intended for qualified investors only. Alternative Investments such as derivatives, hedge funds, private credit, private equity funds, and funds of funds can result

in higher return potential but also higher loss potential. Changes in economic conditions or other circumstances may adversely affect your investments. Before you invest in alternative
investments, you should consider your overall financial situation, how much money you have to invest, your need for liquidity, and your tolerance for risk.

IN THIS ISSUE
Macro Strategy—Superintelligence and Superabundance in the Next Decade:
Rapid advances in Artificial Intelligence (AI) have prompted prominent architects of
the new economy to conclude the Singularity is arriving ahead of schedule as signs
of its effects proliferate in the multitude of new AI models running in the increasing

number of data centers driving one of the biggest investment booms in U.S. history.
Superintelligence that surpasses collective human intelligence has long been one of the
predictions of futurists analyzing the implications of the Singularity. Superabundance is
one of the results of Superintelligence predicted by those who have written about the
consequences of this major acceleration in technological innovation. The early arrival of
these radical forces for change poses imminent challenges for society and investors that

will shape the next decade in critical ways.
In particular, the heightened uncertainty about how this new era plays out increases the
utility of a well-diversified approach to wealth management in our view.
Market View—Elevated Debt Supply Is Testing the Market's Appetite: The recent
rise in Treasury yields reflects a confluence of factors, including Federal Reserve (Fed)
policy uncertainty, deteriorating fiscal dynamics, resilient economic growth, persistent

inflation concerns and a record wave of debt issuance from both the U.S. Treasury and
corporate borrowers, particularly in the long end of the curve. Importantly, we view this
as a period of market adjustment rather than a cause for alarm.
Episodes of debt pressures can be a natural consequence of elevated supply, in addition
to other external factors and could be resolved through higher yields that help balance
supply and demand. While interest rate volatility may remain elevated and long-term

yields could settle at structurally higher levels, today's combination of attractive nominal
and real yields presents a potential opportunity for investors to extend beyond cash and
short-duration holdings and move closer to strategic duration targets.
The Ticker Tape—Top Questions Answered Directly by Our Chief Investment
Officer (CIO).
